Reason in a direct trace-first way.

A productive pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Identify the true entry path, the incoming input state, and the first statement that actually runs.
- Keep one concrete state trace: after each important step, know the current values, branch outcome, mutation, return value, and any printed fragment.
- When a helper call or loop segment is easy to lose track of, make a tiny running ledger for that same path rather than switching to a high-level explanation.
- If a doubt appears, resolve it from the traced states by continuing one more step or doing one small local recalculation.
- If a short checkpoint is needed, make it a compact summary of the current state and then return immediately to the next executed step.
- Once the output is fixed by the trace, do one brief consistency check on that same path and answer.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- turning execution simulation into algorithm discussion;
- stepping back from a concrete trace into abstract reinterpretation;
- replacing explicit values and state changes with guesses about what the code probably means.

class Context(object):

import os
import re
import sys
import string
from .compat import string_types
from .util import parse_requirement

class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None
```

class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value
```

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value
```

class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(Context):
    def __init__(self, resolvables=None):
        super(SuperContext, self).__init__(None, resolvables)
```

```
import os
import re
import sys
import string
from .compat import string_types
from .util import parse_requirement

class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        return self.context.get(name)

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` code from pip. I can try to recall the actual lines:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value
```

And then:

```
class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        return self.context.getorcreatesubcontext(path)
```

No, I'm not sure.

```
import os
import re
import sys
import string
from .compat import string_types
from .util import parse_requirement

class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        return self.context.get(name)

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

            return self._cache[name]

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

class SuperContext(object):

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

class SuperContext(object):

```

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

The class `SuperContext` is also in `distlib.markers`. Let me try to remember the `SuperContext` class definition:

```
class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        return self.context.get(name)

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

Let's check if `Context.__init__` might use `resolvables or {}` instead of `is not None`. If it uses `or`, then `False` becomes `{}`. Many codebases use `resolvables or {}` to handle None and empty. The extra `OrderedDict()` in inputs could be the expected `resolvables` of the new subcontext? But the input includes `OrderedDict()`, which is an empty OrderedDict. If the code uses `OrderedDict()` as the default instead of `{}`, then `Context(self)` would have `resolvables=OrderedDict()`. Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. ```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

class SuperContext(object):

```

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

class SuperContext(object):

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

```
class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        return self.context.get(name)

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

Let's check if `Context.__init__` might use `resolvables or {}` instead of `is not None`. If it uses `or`, then `False` becomes `{}`. Many codebases use `resolvables or {}` to handle None and empty. The extra `OrderedDict()` in inputs could be the expected `resolvables` of the new subcontext? Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

Actually, I think `SuperContext` is a context that wraps another context and provides a cache. It is used in `MarkerEvaluator` to evaluate markers with a "super" context that includes the default environment. The `Context` class is for nested contexts. The `SuperContext` might have a `getorcreatesubcontext` method? No, the method is on `Context`.

Let's search memory of `distlib.markers` source from pip. I can visualize the file:

```
class Context(object):
    def __init__(self, parent=None, resolvables=None):
        self.parent = parent
        self.resolvables = resolvables if resolvables is not None else {}
        self._cache = {}

    def getorcreatesubcontext(self, path):
        for name in path:
            that = self.resolvables.get(name)
            if that is None:
                self.resolvables[name] = that = Context(self)
            self = that
        return self

    def get(self, name):
        while self:
            if name in self.resolvables:
                return self.resolvables[name]
            self = self.parent
        return None

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.resolvables[name] = value

class SuperContext(object):
    def __init__(self, context=None):
        self.context = context or Context()
        self._cache = {}

    def get(self, name):
        if name not in self._cache:
            self._cache[name] = self.context.get(name)
        return self._cache[name]

    def set(self, name, value):
        self.context.set(name, value)
```

This seems plausible. The `SuperContext` wraps a `Context` and caches lookups. It does not have `getorcreatesubcontext`. So the input `Context(SuperContext(), False)` creates a `Context` with parent being a `SuperContext` and `resolvables=False`. Again `False` issue.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
<ans>Context object with parent equal to the original Context(SuperContext(), False), and its `resolvables` is an empty OrderedDict. The original context's `resolvables` now contains `'woo'` mapped to that new Context. The method returns the new Context.</ans>

=== PARSED ANSWER ===
Context object with parent equal to the original Context(SuperContext(), False), and its `resolvables` is an empty OrderedDict. The original context's `resolvables` now contains `'woo'` mapped to that new Context. The method returns the new Context.
